Appendices
A Point-process background
This appendix defines the terms the paper uses for arrival streams: the Poisson, renewal and Hawkes
processes, the conditional intensity, the branching ratio, the Fano factor and the Hurst exponent, and
what it means to fit a Hawkes process. It assumes no prior knowledge of point processes. Readers
who know what a conditional intensity is can go straight to Section A.7. Two notational points: τ is
a bin width for counting arrivals, and T is reserved, as in the rest of the paper, for the service time
of a receiver.
A.1 The Poisson process
The setup. Watch packets arrive from a market-data feed and write down the time of each arrival:
09:30:00.213 <- 1st arrival
09:30:00.451 <- 2nd
09:30:00.802 <- 3rd
09:30:01.014 <- 4th
09:30:01.317 <- 5th
...
That list of timestamps is the object being modelled. Nothing else — no prices, no sides, only when.
The Poisson idea. Suppose that in every very small slice of time there is a small, independent
chance of an arrival. Independent means that whether an arrival happens now does not depend on
when the previous one came, on how many came in the last minute, or on anything else about the
past. The rate of the process is a single number, written µ, in arrivals per second. At a rate of
300 per second, on average 300 arrivals occur in each second. Three consequences follow from the
independence.
(1) The gap between consecutive arrivals is exponentially distributed with mean 1/µ.
At µ = 300 per second the mean gap is
mean gap = 1 µ = 1 300/s ≈ 3.3 ms.
The gap distribution is memoryless: however long one has been waiting, the expected time to the
next arrival is still 3.3ms. The probability that a gap exceeds ∆ is
Pr(gap > ∆) = e−µ ∆,
so at this rate Pr(gap > 3.3 ms) ≈ 36.8%, Pr(gap > 6.6 ms) ≈ 13.5% and Pr(gap > 10 ms) ≈ 5.0%.
The probability that a gap is shorter than a tenth of the mean is
Pr
 
gap < 1 10 mean  = 1 − e−0.1 ≈ 9.52%,
whatever the rate. This is the Poisson benchmark used in Section 3.2 for how often two arrivals
come nearly back to back; on the corpus the share is 73%.
110

=== page 111 ===
Figure 30. The exponential density of interarrival gaps for a Poisson process at 300 arrivals per second.
Dashed vertical lines mark the median, p90 and p99. Half the gaps are shorter than the median of 2.3ms and
10% are longer than 7.7ms. The CME packet stream has a different shape: far more mass near zero and a
much heavier right tail (Figure 6).
(2) The count in any bin of width τ is Poisson-distributed. Let C be the number of arrivals
in a bin of width τ. Then
E[C] = µτ, Var[C] = µτ,
so the ratio of variance to mean is 1 for every τ . This ratio is the Fano factor (Section A.11); a
value other than 1 rejects Poisson.
(3) Counts in disjoint bins are independent. The number of arrivals in [0, 1 s] says nothing
about the number in [1 s, 2 s].
Poisson as the null hypothesis. Poisson is the stream with no memory, no clustering and no
regularity: arrivals are as uncoordinated as possible. A departure from Poisson is structure in the
arrival stream. Identifying where that structure comes from is a separate question, which for this
feed the paper takes up in Sections 4.3 and 5.
Two ways a real feed departs from Poisson. The first is bunching: many gaps are far shorter
than the exponential law predicts. On the corpus 73% of packet gaps are shorter than a tenth of the
mean gap, against 9.52% for Poisson. The second is memory in the rate: after a burst starts, more
arrivals are likely to follow. Modelling either needs a process whose rate can change with its own
history, which is a conditional-intensity point process.
Notation. Some authors write the Poisson rate as λ. Here µ is the background rate of a Hawkes
process, which reduces to a Poisson process when there is no excitation, and λ(t) is the general,
history-dependent intensity. For a Poisson process λ(t) = µ at every t.
A.2 Points, processes and marks
• A point is a single instant at which something happens: in this paper, the timestamp of one
packet (or, in Section 4.3, of one matching-engine transaction).
111

=== page 112 ===
• A process is a random object that unfolds in time; here, a random list of arrival times
t1 < t2 < t3 < · · ·.
• A point process is a random sequence of points on the time axis, specified by when events
happen and nothing else.
If each event carries additional information (message count, side, price, size), the process is a marked
point process: the timestamps are the points and the extra information is the mark. The paper’s
arrival streams are marked — the span of a packet (Section 7.6) is a mark — but the diagnostics of
Section 3.2 use the timestamps alone. Other names in the literature are counting process (emphasising
N (t)), temporal point process (as against spatial), renewal process and self-exciting process, the last
two being particular classes (Section A.7).
Illustrative example. Four MBO messages in the first second of a session might read
09:30:00.001234567 Add, bid 21432.50, size 3
09:30:00.001234571 Add, ask 21432.75, size 5
09:30:00.017891234 Cancel, order X, bid 21432.25
09:30:00.238491702 Trade, ask 21432.75, size 2
The point process is the four timestamps; message type, side, price and size are marks.
A.3 The probability space
An arrival stream is modelled as a stochastic process, a family of random variables indexed by
time, on a probability space (Ω, F , Pr):
• Ω, the sample space, is the set of all possible outcomes; here an outcome is one complete
realisation of a session’s arrivals.
• F , the σ-algebra, is the collection of yes-or-no questions about the outcome to which a
probability can be assigned, such as “did at least one packet arrive between 09:30:00 and
09:30:01?”. It is closed under negation and countable union; for timestamps it is the standard
Borel σ-algebra.
• Pr : F → [0, 1] assigns a probability to each such event, with Pr(∅) = 0, Pr(Ω) = 1 and
countable additivity over disjoint events.
A.4 Filtration: information accumulated over time
A filtration is an increasing family of σ-algebras
{Ft}t≥0, Fs ⊂ Ft for s ≤ t.
Ft is everything observable up to and including time t, and Ft− everything observable strictly before
t. “Conditional on Ft− ” means “given everything seen strictly before t”. A process is adapted to the
filtration if its value at every t is knowable from the history up to t.
112

=== page 113 ===
A.5 The counting process N (t)
The observable object is the random step function
N (t) = #{ i : ti ≤ t },
which starts at N (0) = 0, is non-decreasing and right-continuous, and jumps by one at each arrival.
The number of arrivals in the half-open interval (t, t + δ] is N (t + δ) − N (t).
A.6 Conditional intensity
At time t, ask for the probability that at least one arrival occurs in the next short interval of length
δ, given everything seen so far. The conditional intensity is that probability divided by δ, in the
limit of small δ:
λ(t | Ft− ) = lim δ↓0
1
δ Pr
 
N (t + δ) − N (t) ≥ 1
 Ft−  .
It is the instantaneous arrival rate given the history, in arrivals per second. At an intensity of 300
per second the probability of an arrival in the next millisecond is about λ(t) δ = 300 × 0.001 = 0.3.
For small δ,
Pr(arrival in (t, t + δ]) ≈ λ(t) δ, E[N (b) − N (a)] = E
Z b
a
λ(s) ds.
Specifying λ(·) as a function of the history specifies the point process completely. For a Poisson
process λ(t) = µ and the history carries no information. For a Hawkes process the intensity is the
background µ plus a decaying contribution ϕ(t − ti) from each past arrival (Section A.7).
A.7 Three canonical models: Poisson, renewal and Hawkes
(a) Homogeneous Poisson. λ(t) = µ > 0. Gaps are independent and exponential with mean
1/µ; the count in a bin of width τ is Poisson(µτ ). Every summary statistic takes its reference value:
the coefficient of variation of the gaps is 1, the Fano factor is 1 at every τ , the Hurst exponent is 0.5,
and the gap autocorrelation is zero at every lag.
(b) Renewal process. Gaps ∆i = ti − ti−1 are independent draws from a common distribution
G, not necessarily exponential. The intensity depends only on the time since the last arrival,
λ(t) = r(t − tlast), with r the hazard function of G. A renewal process is Poisson only when G is
exponential. A heavy-tailed G produces many short gaps and a Fano factor well above 1, but the Fano
factor levels off at large τ (at the squared coefficient of variation of G, when that is finite) instead of
growing, and the gaps are uncorrelated. Permuting the gap sequence at random (a Fisher–Yates
shuffle) leaves a renewal process statistically unchanged. The gap shuffle G of Section 3.6 is therefore
a renewal stream with the real gap distribution, and comparing a statistic on the real stream and on
its shuffle separates what the order of the gaps carries from what their lengths carry.
(c) Hawkes (self-exciting) process. Each past arrival raises the intensity for a while:
λ(t) = µ +
X
ti<t
ϕ(t − ti).
µ ≥ 0 is the background rate, the intensity with no prior arrivals. ϕ : (0, ∞) → [0, ∞) is the
kernel: an arrival u seconds ago still contributes ϕ(u) to the intensity now. The kernel is the shape
113

=== page 114 ===
of one event’s influence on the future. Dropping several stones into a pond is the usual picture: the
disturbance at any moment is the sum of the ripples still spreading, each faded by the time since its
stone. Two conditions apply: ϕ is non-negative, since in a self-exciting model a past event can only
raise future intensity, and its integral n =
R
ϕ is finite, so that the past does not accumulate without
bound.
Kernel families. The exponential kernel ϕ(u) = α e−βu has two parameters: α ≥ 0, the jump in
intensity immediately after an arrival, and β > 0, the decay rate, whose inverse 1/β is the kernel’s
decay time. It is the kernel fitted in the paper. It is Markovian: the current intensity summarises
everything needed from the past. Right after an arrival the intensity rises by α; between arrivals
it relaxes toward µ as µ + (λ − µ)e−β∆t; so it can be updated in constant time per arrival. The
power-law kernel ϕ(u) = a (u + c)−(1+p), p > 0, decays more slowly; Hardiman et al. [27] find
power-law kernels on E-mini S&P mid-price changes over lags from seconds to days. A power-law fit
on this corpus is listed in Section 14.
A.8 Branching ratio n
n =
Z ∞
0
ϕ(u) du, n = α/β for the exponential kernel.
n is the mean number of arrivals that one arrival triggers directly. The Hawkes process is equivalently
a branching process (a Galton–Watson process): every arrival is either an immigrant, generated by
the background rate µ, or the child of an earlier arrival, and each arrival has on average n children.
The expected size of the cluster descended from one immigrant is the geometric series
1 + n + n2 + n3 + · · · = 1 1 − n (n < 1),
and the distribution of cluster sizes is the Borel distribution of Appendix B. For n < 1 the process is
subcritical: clusters are finite and the long-run rate is µ/(1 − n). As n → 1 it becomes near-critical:
cluster sizes and durations grow without bound and long-range dependence appears. For n ≥ 1 it is
critical or supercritical: with positive probability one arrival starts a cascade that never ends, and
the rate is not stationary.
On this corpus the exponential-kernel fit gives n = 0.798 on packets and 0.795 on transactions,
with a kernel decay time 1/β of about 167 µs (Table 1). Filimonov and Sornette [24] and Hardiman
et al. [27] report values near 0.9 and near 1 on E-mini S&P mid-price changes; Filimonov and
Sornette [25] show that a fit with a constant background over a window in which the background
varies overstates n.
The critical boundary in practice. At n = 1 the stationary rate µ/(1 − n) is infinite. On a finite
window the process still fires finitely often, but the variance of the count grows faster than linearly
with the window. A maximum-likelihood fit that lands at n = 1 on real data signals non-stationarity,
a mis-specified kernel or a numerical problem rather than a genuinely critical market. On this corpus
one window of 3512 is pinned at that boundary.
A.9 Simulated Hawkes processes across rate and branching ratio
The mean rate ¯λ = µ/(1 − n) and the branching ratio n are the two parameters by which Section 4.9
conditions the tail. They look different in an event raster. Figure 31 simulates an exponential-kernel
Hawkes process over 15 seconds at each of nine combinations, ¯λ ∈ {2, 10, 50} events per second and
114

=== page 115 ===
n ∈ {0.30, 0.60, 0.90}, with a kernel decay time of one second. At low rate and low branching ratio
the stream is nearly Poisson: ticks spread evenly, a straight count curve, a flat intensity. At high
rate and low branching ratio it is dense but still nearly Poisson. At low rate and high branching
ratio the mean is 2 per second but arrivals come in unmistakable bursts, with long empty stretches
between them and the intensity reaching 20–30 per second inside a burst. At high rate and high
branching ratio the intensity swings by an order of magnitude within the window.
Figure 31. Simulated exponential-kernel Hawkes processes. Each cell stacks the event raster (top), the
cumulative count N (t) (middle) and the intensity λ(t) (bottom). Rows: mean rate ¯λ ∈ {2, 10, 50} events per
second. Columns: branching ratio n ∈ {0.30, 0.60, 0.90}. Ogata thinning over 15s with β = 1/s; generator
make_hawkes_grid.py, seed fixed per cell. The time scale is chosen for legibility; the kernel fitted on the
corpus decays about four orders of magnitude faster.
115

=== page 116 ===
A.10 Three signatures that separate Poisson, renewal and Hawkes processes
(i) An excess of short gaps. On the corpus 73% of packet gaps are shorter than a tenth of the mean
gap; any exponential gives 9.52%. A Hawkes process produces such an excess, but so does a renewal
process with a heavy-tailed gap distribution, so this signature rejects Poisson without identifying
self-excitation. On this feed the gap shuffle keeps the whole excess (Table 1), and Section 5 traces
the tightest gaps to the publisher sending consecutive transactions one period apart.
(ii) Correlated gaps. Under self-excitation, short gaps tend to follow short gaps and long gaps
follow long ones, so the gap autocorrelation is positive. A renewal process has independent gaps and
zero autocorrelation at every lag. A rate that varies slowly over the window also produces positive
gap correlation; the one-second-binned null B of Section 3.6 separates that case.
(iii) Burstiness that grows with the time scale. The Fano factor F (τ ) rises with τ on a Hawkes
process and levels off on a Poisson or renewal process. Clustering is then not a feature of one time
scale but looks similar over decades of τ . A slowly varying rate can also make F (τ ) rise, which is
the effect Filimonov and Sornette [25] warn about.
A.11 Fano factor
For a bin width τ , cut the observation interval into K disjoint bins and let Ck = N (kτ ) − N ((k − 1)τ )
be the count in bin k. The Fano factor is
F (τ ) = Var[Ck] E[Ck] .
For a Poisson process F (τ ) = 1 at every τ . For a renewal process with finite-variance gaps F (τ )
tends to the squared coefficient of variation of the gaps as τ grows, a constant. For a clustered
process F (τ ) > 1 and, under self-excitation or a varying rate, it keeps growing with τ . The measure
is named after U. Fano, who introduced it in 1947 for fluctuations in counts of ions. On the corpus
F (τ ) grows from 52 at 0.1s to about a thousand at 60s, and the shuffled stream is flat at about 17
(Table 1).
116

=== page 117 ===
Figure 32. Four simulated processes at the same mean rate of about 20 per second. Top row (F ≈ 0, highly
regular): near-lattice arrivals, nearly equal bin counts. Second row (F ≈ 1, Poisson): the reference. Third row
(F ≈ 3, moderate Hawkes): visible clumping. Bottom row (F ≈ 14, near-critical Hawkes): pronounced bursts
and long quiet stretches, bin counts from near zero to over 50.
Figure 33. Distribution of arrivals per one-second bin (bars) for the four processes of Figure 32, with the
Poisson distribution at the same mean overlaid (curve). F ≈ 0: narrower than Poisson. F ≈ 1: matches
Poisson. F > 1: wider than Poisson, with extra mass in both tails. The NQ packet stream of the corpus has
F (1 s) = 108 (Table 1), far wider than any panel shown.
A.12 Hurst exponent from the scaling of the Fano factor
When the Fano factor grows as a power of the bin width,
F (τ ) ∼ τ 2H−1,
117

=== page 118 ===
the exponent H is the Hurst exponent. H = 0.5 means no long-range dependence (Poisson, and
renewal with finite-variance gaps); 0.5 < H < 1 means long-range dependence, with clustering that
looks similar across time scales; H near 1 means very long memory. It is estimated by least squares
on the log-log plot,
log F (τ ) = (2H − 1) log τ + c + ε,
so H = (slope + 1)/2. On the corpus H = 0.74 (Table 1).
Figure 34. F (τ ) against τ on logarithmic axes for four simulated processes. Regular: F near zero. Poisson:
flat at 1. Moderate Hawkes: rising. Near-critical Hawkes: steeper rise, with slope about 0.5, i.e. H ≈ 0.75.
Shuffling the gap sequence preserves the gap distribution exactly and destroys the ordering, and
with it any long-range dependence: on the shuffled stream H → 0.5. On the corpus the shuffle takes
H from 0.74 to 0.50, so the long-range dependence lives in the order of the gaps (Section 3.2).
A.13 Fitting an exponential-kernel Hawkes process
The input is a sorted list of arrival times t1 < · · · < tm over a window [0, tend], and there are three
parameters:
• µ, the background rate: arrivals per second with no recent history;
• α, the jump: the rise in intensity, in arrivals per second, at each arrival;
• β, the decay rate: each jump fades as e−βu, with decay time 1/β.
Fitting means choosing the parameters under which the observed timestamps are most probable, i.e.
maximising the log-likelihood [23]
log L(µ, α, β) =
mX
i=1
log λ(ti) −
Z tend
0
λ(s) ds. (13)
The first term rewards high intensity at the times arrivals actually occurred; the second penalises a
model that predicts more arrivals than occurred. If α is too small the model cannot explain the
118

=== page 119 ===
bursts; if α approaches β it predicts more clustering than there is; a wrong µ mis-explains the quiet
periods.
For the exponential kernel both terms have closed forms computable in one pass. With
Ri = e−β(ti−ti−1)(1 + Ri−1), R1 ≡ 0,
the intensity at each arrival is λ(ti) = µ + αRi, and the compensator is
Z tend
0
λ(s) ds = µ tend + α β mX i=1   1 − e−β(tend−ti) .
Timestamps are shifted to the start of the window before fitting, so that the exponentials do not
underflow. The paper maximises the log-likelihood per window with Nelder–Mead over (µ, α, β) and
checks the optimum with a reparameterised L-BFGS-B fit (Section 3.2). From the fitted parameters
follow the branching ratio n = α/β, the kernel decay time 1/β, and the long-run rate ¯λ = µ/(1 − n):
the background rate amplified by 1/(1 − n) through self-excitation.

B The Borel batch-size tail at fixed branching ratio
This appendix records the calculation behind Equation (8) and the numerical check that the critical
k−1/2 survival tail of the Borel distribution is confined to a finite window at every n < 1.
Stirling’s formula k! =
√
2πk kke−k (1 + O(1/k)) in Equation (7) gives
Pr(K = k) = (nk)k−1e−nk
k! = nk−1kk−1e−nk √ 2πk kke−k (1 + O(1/k)) = 1 n √ 2π k−3/2   ne1−nk (1 + O(1/k)).
Writing n = 1 − ϵ, log(ne1−n) = log(1 − ϵ) + ϵ = −ϵ2/2 − ϵ3/3 − · · · , so the geometric factor is
exp(−kϵ2/2 (1 + O(ϵ))). The pmf is therefore ∝ k−3/2 for k ≪ k⋆ = ϵ−2 and geometric beyond;
summing, Pr(K > k) ≍ k−1/2 on the same window and ≍ k−3/2e−kϵ2/2 beyond it. At n = 1 exactly,
119

=== page 120 ===
k⋆ = ∞, Pr(K > k) ∼ p 2/π k−1/2 (Borel–Tanner), and E[K] = ∞; that is the unstable case ρ = ∞,
which no stationary queue attains.
Because the tail is geometric, K has finite exponential moments E[eθK ] < ∞ for θ < ϵ2/2 (1+O(ϵ))
and is therefore outside the subexponential class S. Heavy-tail queueing results that assume
subexponential batch work, including the single-big-batch principle for M [K]/G/1 and the monotone-
separable tail theorem of Baccelli et al. [14], do not apply, and the stationary waiting time of the
batch-arrival queue has a Cramér–Lundberg (exponential) tail at every fixed n < 1.

successive k, which a pure k−3/2 law would hold at −1.5.

the reason the n = 0.80 row of Table 31 is the relevant one.

C Numerical check of Theorem 1
A direct simulation of the model of Theorem 1 checks both the identity Equation (4) and the bound
Equation (6). Immigrants arrive as a Poisson process at rate µ = 0.004 µs−1, each spawning a Galton–
Watson cluster with Poisson(0.9) offspring, so K ∼ Borel(0.9) and E[K] ≈ 10; clusters are placed as
instantaneous batches, giving ρ = ¯λT ≈ 0.40 at T = 10 µs. The tandem has N = 4 equal stages
of 2.5 µs and h = 1.7 µs; 2 × 105 messages are simulated with an explicit per-stage FIFO recursion
(independent of the paper’s sweep code). Over all messages, maxj |W (4) j − (wj (T /4) + 3h)| < 10−9 µs
and Equation (6) is violated by no message. The quantiles are
q W (1) q W (4) q W (1) q /4 + 3h (W (4) q − 3h)/W (1) q
0.5 350.0 55.1 92.6 0.14
0.9 2066.6 355.1 521.8 0.17
0.99 5342.1 1127.6 1340.6 0.21
0.999 7586.2 1585.1 1901.7 0.21
in microseconds. The tandem lands below the bound at every quantile, with the ratio in the
last column between 0.14 and 0.21 against the bound’s 0.25; the slack is the ρ/N utilisation effect
described in the remark after Theorem 1. Under the Poisson null at the same message rate (ρ = 0.40,
which is far above the corpus utilisation and is used here only to make the Poisson wait visible)
the single-stage wait quantiles at q = 0.9, 0.99, 0.999 are 10.3, 24.8, 38.5 µs and the tandem’s are
5.1, 7.5, 9.2 µs, again inside the bound. At the corpus utilisation (ρ = 0.0045 at this appendix’s
T = 10 µs) the same two collapse to 0 and 3h at q = 0.9 and q = 0.99, which is Corollary 2; at
q = 0.999 they do not, since ρ there exceeds 1 − q = 0.001 and the corollary does not apply.

E A synthetic feed on which the tail at 8 µs depends on intensity
and branching ratio
Section 4.9 finds the normalised tail on CME MDP3 invariant to packet rate and branching ratio
at HFT service times. This appendix constructs, with the same simulator, a feed on which both
dependences appear at T = 8 µs, and identifies what distinguishes it from CME.
Arrivals are generated as a Hawkes process through its cluster representation: immigrants arrive
as a Poisson process of rate µ = ¯λ(1 − n), and each spawns a Galton–Watson tree with Poisson(n)
offspring at exponential delays of mean 1/β. Three conditions are run. In the first the kernel is fast,
1/β = 1 µs, and there is no minimum period. In the second the same fast kernel is followed by a
minimum period equal to the publisher period, spub = 7.5 µs: each arrival is delayed until at least
spub after its predecessor, modelling the exchange’s publisher (Section 5.4). In the third the kernel
is slow, 1/β = 80 µs, with the same minimum period; this is the condition closest to the CME gap
structure, though the kernel fitted on the corpus is slower still, about 160 µs (Section 3.2). The grid
is n ∈ {0.5, 0.7, 0.8, 0.9, 0.95} and ¯λ ∈ {500, 2000, 8000, 32000, 64000} packets per second, so that
ρ = ¯λT at T = 8 µs runs from 0.004 to 0.51; 2 × 105 events per cell, three seeds, median reported.
The last column of Table 32 is the fraction of interarrival gaps at the minimum period, measured at
¯λ = 8000; on the CME corpus 2.15% of packet gaps are shorter than 7.9 µs, just above the publisher
period (Table 6).

Table 32. Synthetic Hawkes feeds at the sweep point just above the publisher period, T = 8 µs: p99/T by
branching ratio and packet rate, for three kernel and minimum-period conditions and a Poisson stream; the
last column is the share of gaps at the minimum period. The ρ row gives the utilisation. What the table shows:
on every synthetic feed the tail grows steeply with the branching ratio, and the share of gaps at the minimum
period grows with it; the Poisson stream has a tail only at high utilisation. What follows: on these feeds the
branching ratio matters because it controls how many arrivals are packed at the minimum period, which on
CME the publisher fixes. Figure 36 shows the same numbers as bars.

Figure 36. Table 32 as bars, log scale, for branching ratio 0.8: the normalised tail p99/T at T = 8 µs on
synthetic feeds, by packet rate. A fast kernel with no minimum period (pink) has a tail of about a hundred
times T ; adding a minimum period equal to the publisher period (vermillion) cuts it more than tenfold; a slow
kernel with the minimum period (green) is lower still, closer to the Poisson stream (black). What sets the tail
at 8 µs is how many gaps sit at the minimum period, which the kernel and the minimum period control and
the branching ratio alone does not.
Three observations.
Intensity. In every synthetic Hawkes condition the tail does not change with the packet rate while
utilisation stays low, and it rises once utilisation reaches a few percent, steeply by the time the server
is half busy. The Poisson row rises earlier, because for it there is nothing but utilisation. The CME
corpus, at the sweep point just above the publisher period, sits deep inside the flat region even in
its busiest windows. The invariance to rate found in Section 4.9 is therefore what a Hawkes model
predicts at that utilisation, and it ends where utilisation queueing begins, as the CME sweep also
finds at long service times.
Criticality. The synthetic feeds depend strongly on the branching ratio in every condition: the tail
grows by about two orders of magnitude from the least to the most self-exciting feed, and roughly
doubles between the two values nearest the corpus’s. The CME corpus shows no dependence over its
own, narrow, range. The last column of the table explains the difference. A synthetic Hawkes feed
at the corpus’s branching ratio places between a quarter and four fifths of its gaps at the minimum
period, depending on the kernel; on CME only about one gap in fifty is that short. On the synthetic
feeds the branching ratio controls how many arrivals are packed at the minimum period, and that
packing is what queues on a server at the production service time. On CME the packed fraction is
set by the publisher and does not vary with the fitted branching ratio; the fitted ratio describes the
ordering of arrivals at longer timescales, consistent with Section 3.2, where the corpus is found to be
self-exciting with heavy-tailed gaps rather than a pure exponential-kernel Hawkes process.
Feed dependence of the design rule. At the sweep point just above the publisher period, at
the corpus’s branching ratio and a moderate packet rate, a two-stage tandem has the lower p99 on
every synthetic condition: it halves the tail on the feed with no minimum period, and on the two
feeds with a minimum period it takes a tail of several times the service time down to almost nothing.
124

=== page 125 ===
On CME at the same service time one stage is best (Section 4.8). The service time at which splitting
begins to pay is not a constant; it is a property of the feed.
Summary. Whether splitting a stage reduces its tail depends on how many packets arrive closer
together than the stage’s service time, not on the packet rate or the branching ratio as such. On
CME about 2% of packet gaps are shorter than 7.9 µs, just above the publisher period. That fraction
does not change when the market becomes busier, since a busier market produces more clusters with
the same spacing inside them, nor when the fitted branching ratio moves within its observed range.
At the 7–8 µs service time of a production decode path there is therefore little to queue behind across
packets, and one thread is the right design as far as that queue goes; the tail from long packets and
variable service at that service time is separate (Sections 7.7 and 7.8). On a feed that places a large
share of its gaps at the minimum period, the result inverts: a Hawkes process at the same n = 0.8
places between a quarter and four fifths of its gaps at the minimum period instead of about 2%,
the tail depends strongly on the branching ratio, splitting reduces the tail at T = 8 µs where on
CME it does not, and at high enough load the packet rate matters as well. The Poisson null shows
only the load effect, and only at high utilisation, as a null should. The rule is therefore neither one
thread nor two. The split threshold, the service time above which cutting a stage lowers its p99, is
a property of the feed’s publisher period; it cannot be read off the fitted parameters, and it must
be measured under the load the system will see. On the CME feed a 7 µs stage sits below the split
threshold of 9–10 µs; another exchange, or the same exchange after a change to its publisher, may
not. Section 12 states this as a design principle.
